But the most disappointing thing about all this is that the institute faced these economic problems with tens of millions of euros in its accounts and, according to the ministry itself, they could not be easily accessed. A major misconception, Minister. Public research agencies have seen a considerable increase in their investment, which we applaud, but you still do not face the structural change needed to get rid of the administrative obstacles and legislative constraints that make your work very difficult. For example, whenever a public research body takes a decision to participate in a European initiative, the approval of the subscription fee must pass through the Council of Ministers. Yes, ladies and gentlemen, you have heard well, by the Council of Ministers, even if it is a minimum amount of hundreds of euros. On many occasions these amounts remain unpaid, causing significant damage to our scientific entities. Likewise, if they want to hire staff and have the funds approved by the IGAE, the awards have to go through the approval of the Undersecretary of State. They hear well again, by the Undersecretary of State. If they are not able to make the scientific ecosystem more flexible, they will not be able to make it operational, and even if they drain European funds, we will not be able to achieve anything. ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u149	Thank you, Madam President. Good afternoon, ladies and gentlemen. Today we are discussing in this House general state budgets that are opening a new stage in Spain, from the economic and also from the political point of view. These are historical budgets that definitely leave behind the budgets of precariousness and the cuts of Cristóbal Montoro, which are going to represent the highest level of public investment in history and that will allow us to rebuild public services, recover rights and face the great challenges in terms of transformation that our homeland has. The last budgets that were approved in this House in 2018 during the government of Mariano Rajoy were budgets that were inserted within the framework of policies of neoliberal austerity, dismantling of public services and erosion of acquired labour, social and pension rights; a political stage marked by corruption, which resulted in a serious deterioration of the living conditions of the social majority. It should be remembered especially in this year of pandemic that the cuts of more than EUR 10 billion in public health - which everyone today understands that we were placed in a situation of greater weakness to deal with a health emergency like the one we are experiencing - were a mistake. While in Europe there was an increase in health investment in the same years, in Spain the gross domestic product was reduced by almost 1 percentage point. I think it is clear that if these cuts in public health had not been carried out, our country would have been able to cope with the COVID-19 pandemic in many better conditions. It would be no more for the People's Party to recognize here that it was wrong to cut public health and assume that this is something that never again, ever again, can happen in Spain. I invite you, ladies and gentlemen, to do so; I think it would be a noble exercise of political dignity that many people would like to hear, including many voters of the People's Party. Faced with those cuts in health, today we are discussing budgets that increase public health investment by 150%, which is something that, honestly, I think we should put into value, given the circumstances. I think it's the least we could do for all the doctors, nurses and public health professionals who have played and continue to play the type, in very difficult conditions, to protect us all from the threat of the coronavirus. And beyond health, these are accounts that increase investment in education by 70%; 46 % in dependency; 80 % in research; 22 % in university scholarships; 30 % in employment promotion; 60 % in the fight against child poverty; 70 % in social services; or 157 % in equality policies, among other important data. The policy, ladies and gentlemen, is to a large extent to decide where the money is put, where the resources and collective wealth of a country are put, and this Government is putting the resources to ensure the defence of a fundamental constitutional principle, which is the general interest. Because these data I have listed translate into the fact that boys and girls in Spain are going to have access to quality education, regardless of their economic origin; that our older adults receive worthy attention in line with the efforts they have made throughout their lives; that our scientists and researchers can exercise their profession with more support and resources, without being forced to emigrate so that, in the end, it is other countries that benefit from the fruits of their talent; that if a worker loses his or her job he or she has more support from the institutions; that social services professionals have more tools to guarantee social protection or that public administrations have more instruments and resources to promote full equality between men and women; and also for the eradication of male-female violence. These budgets also contain some measures that I believe it is important to highlight in the area of fiscal justice, which will help to ensure that those who have the most, contribute more, and that they represent a very important first step towards a profound reform of our tax system, which corrects the disadvantage of more than 7 points that we carry with regard to the countries in our environment. In this sense, we reduce the deductions to private pension schemes; we raise company tax for large business groups, limiting the dividend and capital gains exemptions generated by their participation in subsidiary companies; we raise 1 point the property tax for those assets of more than 10 million euros; we raise 3 points the IRPF for capital income of more than 200 000 euros; we raise 2 points the IRPF for labour income of more than 300 000 euros, and we set a minimum tax of 15% for so-called Socimi. Surely the right-wings, who do not believe in the tax justice that our Constitution establishes and who always work to defend the interests of those who have the most, are going to say that these fiscal measures affect the pockets of the middle and working classes, which, as we all know, have salaries in excess of EUR 300 000 and assets of more than EUR 10 million. I will highlight some of the measures that are within the competence of the Vice-President of Social Rights and Agenda 2030 and some other ministerial departments with which we are working to make them effective. First, as noted above, strengthening attention to dependency. We will increase the State's contribution to the dependency system by more than €600 million to reduce waiting lists and improve benefits, reversing the cuts made by the People's Party in 2012. We are going to use more than EUR 700 million of European funds to carry out investments that will improve care for the elderly, moving from the current model, based on residences, towards a model more similar to that of the Nordic countries, which will bet on home care. Secondly, we are going to strengthen the care system and we are going to promote conciliation. We will invest EUR 200 million this year to boost education from 0 to 3 years, EUR 300 million to equalize maternity and paternity leave in sixteen weeks and to make them in transferable and remunerated at 100 per cent, and we will also use EUR 200 million to support the reconciliation of families with children under 14 years of age through the public procurement of professional caregivers, as a first step towards building a care system that assumes the co-responsibility of public administrations in this area and which is also a source of employment generation. I believe that these are very important measures to respond to a crisis that, as the Ministry of Equality often insists, has also been a crisis in the care system. Thirdly, the strengthening of social protection. We strengthen active employment policies with an additional EUR 2 billion; we allocate more than EUR 200 million to strengthen and modernise public social services, as head of the Ministry of Social Rights, on which social services in Spain depend; we ensure the updating of pensions to the CPI and the increase of non-contributory pensions twice the CPI; and we increase by 5% the so-called Iprem, the public indicator of income with multiple effects. For everyone to understand, unemployment benefits and the majority of the social assistance available in Spain are linked to the Iprem, which had been virtually unchanged for ten years. This increase, for example, will improve the benefits that many long-term unemployed are receiving, the active income of insertion, the income of farming, rental aid or a multitude of scholarships. Finally, we cannot talk about these budgets without mentioning the reconstruction agreement reached in Europe, a historic agreement that expresses a new consensus of the time, a new common sense of the time around neo-Keynesian policies as a more effective way of dealing with a reconstruction that can be compared with other moments of European reconstruction, such as the one that took place after the Second World War. I believe that those of us who have been defending for years that there was another way of dealing with a crisis from the European institutions and that the stage of neoliberal austerity has caused serious damage to the common project, we have reason to be very pleased that Europe's response, largely thanks to the work of this government and other governments in southern Europe, is, to a large extent, in line with what some of us have always been betting on. Now we have the challenge as a country to take advantage of this opportunity provided by European funds to move our country forward in a progressive direction, to overcome the structural weaknesses of our economic system and to tackle the major transformations that Spain needs in terms of reindustrialisation, economic diversification, digitisation and ecological transition. He said at first that these budgets are opening a new stage in Spain in economic terms, but also in political terms. The majority that will be expressed here in the next few hours, for which some of us have worked hard not only during the last few months, but during the last few years, is a majority that represents a country project, a plural, diverse, democratic, fraternal and plurinational Spain that I think looks much more like the country that we are than the narrow and centralist idea that has the right. ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u167	Very good afternoon to all your lordships. With these accounts we broke with the dogmas of the 2008 crisis and reversed priorities, neither austerity nor cuts nor privatizations. What Spain, its society and its economy need, what the people of our country need is to reduce the social gap, to reduce the inequalities generated and exacerbated in the last decade, to profoundly transform our economy, to strengthen public services to unburden the weight of women's shoulders and bodies and to leave to the next generations a more just, more sustainable and more feminist society and economy. The data speak for themselves. Social service allocations are growing by more than 70 per cent, this Government is committed to consolidating the fourth pillar of the welfare state, with more attention to dependency, children and adolescents or child poverty, all of which will strengthen a system of public care and will prevent the burden of care and care for the most vulnerable from falling on mothers, grandmothers and precarious professional women. There is also the largest share for scholarships: EUR 2090 million, plus EUR 514 million. Scholarships for the study are a fundamental mechanism for equal opportunities and the elimination of gender barriers in higher education, and show a greater impact among women who access university studies. Health policy is also very important, which will have to strengthen our public health, and particularly health groups, especially women. The unemployment policy will have an additional 4191 million; as you know, according to the latest EPA, women account for 52% of the unemployed in the third quarter of the year, compared to 48% of men. Women are the most punished for the economic effects of the health emergency, as they are the collective that mostly sustains areas such as hospitality, tourism or trade. In short, these are not more budgets, they are budgets to solve the main problems our society has, they are budgets to transform the country from social justice and from feminism. They are, moreover, courageous accounts that dare to tackle and tackle some of the large outstanding debts that this country has, but not with the vulture funds or with the large speculative financial institutions, but with the people and particularly with the women of this country. Ladies and gentlemen, as you know, the section for the Ministry of Equality increases its budget by 157% by 2021 to EUR 451.42 million, EUR 275.89 million more than in the previous budget. We also warmly welcome the fact that, thanks to the push of the feminist movement and the work of the parliamentary groups of the United Nations Podemos and Socialists, we will see an increase of 20 million more for the fight against the violence of machoism from the local entities. The increase in this section translates into three fundamental axes of action: the first, equality between men and women; the second, the fight against sexist violence; and the third, the protection of diversity or ethnic-racial discrimination, or on grounds of sexual orientation or gender identity. With regard to the first axis of action, equality between men and women, the work of the Women's Institute in relation to equality in employment, gender mainstreaming in public policies, feminist research, the impetus to the associative movement or the fight against the educational gap in scientific and technical professions, for naming only some of its most relevant dedications. It is also embodied in a public policy of co-responsibility that families, and particularly women, need in terms of reconciliation and care for children, aggravated - as you well know - by the enormous difficulties resulting from the pandemic faced by women who are, above all, the caretakers. To this end, we have a budget of EUR 200 million, earmarked for what we have called the Correspondent Plan. It will be a new public policy that seeks to guarantee the right to care in Spain, care as a right, and that we understand as a seed policy, as a first step on the road to the construction of a state care system. With these General State Budgets, public policies no longer turn their backs on the entire area of the reproductive economy, which has, so far, always remained blind to public policies in our country. The care crisis, diagnosed by the feminist economy in a remarkable way since the 2008 economic crisis, has intensified in our country due to the health emergency, which has also highlighted the essential importance of care work that has historically fallen on women in an invisible and precarious manner. As expressed in the Reconstruction Commission, it was therefore essential to start the path towards a rethinking of the system that would entail structural change in order to prioritize the sustainability of life, our reality as interdependent human beings, which we need each other, and the binding role that the State must assume as guarantor of dignified care. In this sense, the Correspondent Plan aims to establish mechanisms and lines of action that guarantee the right to care in Spain, and we also aim to do so from a feminist perspective, understanding that the distribution of care is a task and a necessity of first order for women, because, if we do not close the care gap, it will be impossible to achieve full equality between women and men. It seems to us very important to note that, from the Ministry of Equality, we aspire to design the various lines of action to deploy the Co-responsible Plan side by side, with the autonomous communities, with the local entities, over all the autonomous communities, which will be responsible for their management. The guidelines that the Ministry of Equality will propose for the implementation of this Co-responsible Plan include facilitating the reconciliation of personal, family and work life, as provided for in the Equality Act in its article 44, contributing to a more balanced sharing of care responsibilities within families, but especially between families and public administrations; creating employment in the ways that we can agree with the autonomous communities, but which we aim to enable them to move in the public and public-social spheres, and the dignity of care work, in addition to the certification of experience and professional capacity in the field of care, especially for women, who have spent many years of their lives in non-professional care. In addition, awareness of co-responsibility will also be a priority. Since this plan is the most important development in this section, I should like to point out that the promotion of equality continues to count on the indispensable programmes and work carried out by the Women's Institute, whose name, also thanks to these General State Budgets, will become the Women's Institute. The change of name, although it may seem to them somewhat secondary, is important to us and is a historical demand that these budgets finally come to meet. This plural symbolizes the plurality and diversity of women in this country and captures the advances and changes in feminist reflection, which take us away from a unique idea of women as a unique and almost unknown category, to bet on a strong and express recognition of what we are, more than half of the population, more than half of the citizenry. By 2021, this historic and indispensable equality body in Spain maintains its level of mobilization of resources for the development of the policies it has entrusted, some of which detailed them previously. Ladies and gentlemen, let me tell you about our second axis of action, the fight against machist violence, reflected in particular in programme 232.C, actions for the comprehensive prevention of gender-based violence; and also in 230.A, within the Mechanism for Recovery and Resilience. First, I would like to point out to you that stepping up efforts to combat macho violence is a central objective of this Ministry of Equality and of the Government as a whole, paying particular attention to the terrible consequences that the pandemic has had on women victims of such violence. That is why today we can point out that, with the approval of these budgets, we will have reached the highest figure that Spain has ever invested in the fight against macho violence. As we have discussed on other occasions, the pandemic has created a new and unexpected global context with devastating effects for women, including their children, and the response to the emergency by COVID-19 worldwide and specifically in Spain has placed women victims of gender-based violence in a new scenario of vulnerability compounded by the containment measures that have facilitated aggressors to exercise silent, controllable violence and also with more impunity. Restrictions on freedom of movement or the impossibility and difficulty in accessibility and also in the presence of certain central institutions for the accompaniment to these women, as may be the courts, have made it difficult to break the silence and decision-making of women, as has been observed in the increase in calls and in requests for help received in the 016 service during the period of the state of alarm. In addition, ladies and gentlemen, our proposals on violence against women are particularly endorsed and accompanied this time by the 2019 macro-survey. As you will recall, the data he has revealed is simply frightening: one in two women has suffered some kind of macho violence in our country, including, as I say, all forms of macho violence, which go beyond what happens in the context of the couple or the ex-partner. The photograph of the macro-survey, in addition to the international standards to which our country owes itself in this area and also to what has been established by its ladies and gentlemen in the State Pact against Gender Violence adopted in the General Courts, obliges us to take great steps in designing policies to combat gender-based violence. Our commitment, therefore, must be to go beyond violence against women within the framework of the partner or ex-partner. All sexist violence requires assistance, protection and also reparation. Our aim is, therefore, to expand and consolidate comprehensive care and prevention of all forms of violence against women, not only those occurring in the context of the partner or ex-partner. Let all women know that they are not alone and that there are wide and sufficient gates of entry to be believed by all State institutions and to be accompanied in their process of exiting violence and of redressing the damage caused. To that end, we are promoting essential legislative advances, such as the Law on the Comprehensive Guarantee of Sexual Freedom or the Comprehensive Law against Trafficking, and we also believe that policies against sexist violence need a definitive impetus to be institutionalized, that is, so that all competent administrations and institutions at any level of the Administration know what practices, concrete measures, concrete policies should be promoted and promoted to prevent and eradicate violence against women. These General State Budgets include the tools to accompany these legislative and institutional efforts to combat sexist violence in the economic sphere, to fulfil all obligations for all women and to deal with all violence. In this regard, it is very good news that the budget for the fight against gender-based violence amounts to EUR 278 million, thus exceeding the EUR 200 million committed in the economic commitments of the opinion of the State Pact against Gender-based Violence. As I was telling you, there have never been so many resources invested to eradicate violence against women, ever. These are certainly also anti-machism budgets. With regard to the State Pact, the amount corresponding to the Ministry of Equality will finally amount to 200 million euros, thanks to the work of its ladies and gentlemen, and we will also have another 20 million euros for the fight against violence against women, which is part of the budget headings in the Ministries of Justice, Health, the Interior or Labour and the Social Economy. As reflected in the State's General Budgets, 100 million will be transferred to the autonomous communities regarding compliance with the State Pact, 40 million to local entities in the same sense, and the Government's delegation against Gender Violence will be increased to 60 million euros. I would like to pause for a moment to point out that they will have noticed that not all transfers to the autonomous communities of the State Pact are in the same chapter this time. This time 90 million are distributed in Chapter 4 and 10 million in Chapter 6. With this division we fulfil the commitment made by this ministry, by this Government, within the framework of the sectoral conference, a traditional demand by the autonomous communities to be able to dispose of part of these funds for permanent material investments. We trust that this step will be a step forward in further improving good practices resulting from the implementation of the State Pact against Gender Violence by all administrations. [[The Lord]] On the other hand, the Ministry of Equality also presents a project within the Mechanism for Recovery and Resilience: the Plan Spain protects you against macho violence, whose budget line amounts to €48 million in 2021. Under the same mechanism and in collaboration with the Ministry of Labour, we will develop a national plan for the social and labour integration of victims of trafficking, sexual exploitation and women in prostitution. It is a project worth EUR 10 million. I would like to give you some details of the main and most innovative projects to be carried out on this axis. First, the National Strategy for the Eradication of Machist Violence 20212025. This is a new investment project with a budget of more than €4 million, with which we will promote measures to eradicate all forms of violence against women and with which support for victims will be strengthened through the adoption of a comprehensive plan for the compensation, reparation and recovery of autonomy for women who suffer or have suffered any form of macho violence. The strategy is the public policy that will allow us to strengthen and complement the state pact, incorporating measures not present in the pact, such as addressing violence in networks, symbolic violence, all sexual violence, measures to work with children, young people and men, or preventing violence in public spaces or leisure spaces. This strategy will also enable us to implement Spain's responsibilities under the Istanbul Convention and the European Strategy for Gender Equality 20202025. Secondly, a macro-study on trafficking in women and girls. As you know, trafficking for the purpose of sexual exploitation of women and girls is one of the most cruel and violent forms of macho violence and, therefore, the Government considers it essential to recognize and identify the prevalence of this violence and also how many girls and women suffer it in our country through a macro-study that can rigorously show the situation of women and girls who have been trafficked for all their purposes - sexual exploitation is the majority, but not the only one that has a marked gender character - as well as the identification of the route of criminal networks. During the state of alarm between March and June 2020, the Ministry of Equality for the first time promoted social and health accreditation for women victims of this form of sexist violence so that they could have access to their rights, whatever their situation, whatever their administrative situation. We need to have a better picture of what is happening in our country with trafficking and sexual exploitation so that we can fine-tune the instruments that institutions develop and put at the service of these women so that we can help them. Thirdly, the Plan Spain protects you against macho violence, with a budget impact of 48.5 million euros, as I told you before. As I pointed out to them, the health crisis caused by COVID19 had a significant impact on the prevalence of violence against women, which caused specific measures to be taken in this area. In particular, as you know, the contingency plan for victims of gender-based violence, victims of trafficking, sexual exploitation and women in prostitution, through which, among other measures, we declare essential services for the care of victims of gender-based violence, thus ensuring their provision and the safety of the workers who provide them. This socio-sanitary accreditation was also implemented for women victims of trafficking, sexual exploitation and women in prostitution contexts indicated above. The plan - and, specifically, some of the measures envisaged in it - has received congratulations from different international human rights agencies such as the United Nations, UN Women or the Council of Europe, including it as an example of good practices carried out during the COVID pandemic19. The functioning of these services sheds light on the increase in violence in the exceptional context of the pandemic and, for example, the data on information, counselling, care and protection of victims and the management of these services carried out directly by the Ministry of Equality verify the need to further strengthen and improve their functioning as an undoubted alternative to the needs of women and to extend all these resources to all forms of violence against women and not exclusively to intimate partner violence, which has traditionally focused the resources of the Ministry of Equality. The implementation of the measures of the contingency plan also revealed a strong digital gap in the current services that was clearly increased in the face of the restriction of movements foreseen by the state of alarm and, therefore, in order to close both gaps and to predict the resilience of comprehensive care services in the face of future crises, the Plan Spain protects you against macho violence, with the aim of extending and making accessible to all potential victims of all forms of violence against women comprehensive care services. With the funds earmarked for this purpose, the Autonomous Communities will be able to set up the comprehensive care centres 24 hours for victims of sexual violence provided for in the Law on Comprehensive Guarantee of Sexual Freedom. It will also allow us to expand and modernize service 016, adding to the services that already provide an instant messaging service on a permanent basis - as you know, the instant messaging service was something of its own and focused on the state of alarm and we want it to become something permanent - as well as psychological care and emotional containment, socio-labor advice to the victims and monitoring them. An important item is also planned to modernize and improve the care and protection of victims, both the Atenpro service and the so-called bracelets, so that they are more accessible and modern, and can be used by all victims of all forms of violence against women. With this plan, Spain protects you against sexist violence, we will be ready to double the phone and online consultations that are made through 016, including socio-laboral and psychological care; we will be able to reach the 120,000 relevant consultations per year in this service; we will increase the Atenpro care service by more than 30,000 users per year, and we intend to reach the 4000 new annual devices to control the protection measures for women victims. With this plan, we also aspire to the construction of more than fifty comprehensive care centres 24 hours for victims of sexual violence throughout Spain, which will allow us to care for the more than three hundred thousand women who, according to the macro-survey, have suffered some kind of sexual violence only in the last year. With this plan we will fulfil all our obligations for all victims of all violence. Spain will not take a step back against machismo. Finally, this budget sets out the material reality of the Directorates-General for Equal Treatment and Ethnic Diversity, as well as the Directorate for Sexual Diversity and Rights (LGTBI), two management centres of vital importance to this ministry, in a context in which hate speech for racial, sexual orientation and gender identity is increasing in our country and in a global context of the rise of extreme rights. As you know, our commitment to equality is firm, and this will also be reflected in the various rules in this area, which we hope can be adopted next year. Ladies and gentlemen, you will allow me to conclude this statement by reiterating the feminist commitment of this government, which I know is also that of the majority of this House. The commitments serve when accompanied by economic resources, as the feminist movement has always told us very clearly. We know that without that economic commitment, equality policies remain mere declarations of intent. Feminism has been pointing out for decades the crisis of care and the need to distribute wealth, time and care to eradicate inequalities between women and men. Feminism is also responsible for the mobilization that led this House to draft the State Pact against Gender Violence. The Ministry of Equality and the Government of Spain take care of this feminist agenda, which is historic, while radically current and essential for our present and also for tomorrow. We believe that these budgets express our commitment to this agenda, because the bulk of the allocation of this department has to do with the impact, with the real policy, with the coordination between administrations in order to fight against the macho violence and to promote real reconciliation and co-responsibility in the distribution of care. This budget is going to strengthen this scenario of coordinated, collaborative governance between administrations, and I think that is essential, and I want to thank you here today, because it is an added challenge, but at the same time it is the best mechanism we have for making public policies that achieve equality. Ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to thank today, and with this end, the votes in favour of these accounts. These budgets are the transformation tool that Spain needs to emerge from the crisis, but also to face the immediate future with models of justice, rights and guarantees of life worthy of being lived for all and for all. These are the budgets that give continuity to the State Pact against Gender Violence, which allow us to work for equality between men and women, for equality between racialized and collective persons and LGBTI persons. It is the budgets that allow this Government to continue with a feminist agenda, essential in a modern society like ours, which aspires to build a future of social justice. I am convinced that the feminist majority I am talking to today will prevail throughout the entire legislature and will be the one that will allow the greatest and best progress that our society has been demanding for many years on the streets. Thank you so much. [[Prolonged applause]]

For the section presentation, I have the floor on behalf of the Government, the Minister for Consumer Affairs.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u181	Thank you very much, Madam President. Good afternoon to all your lordships. It is a pleasure to come up to this rostrum in order to be able to defend a part of these General Budgets of the State, which are those that need Spain at the moment; which are expansive budgets, in economic terms; which contribute to the deployment of the social shield; which allow us to open the way for the reindustrialization of our productive system, and which, above all, they do is to protect working families. And what belongs to me today is to defend the lines of public policy of my ministry, of the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, which is the ministry I represent, and which already has a mandate in the Spanish Constitution, in its article 51. Article 51 of our Constitution states that the public authorities must ensure the rights of the consumer, they must ensure the protection of these legitimate commercial rights, but also for the safety and, of course, the health of the consumer. All of us, and we, who are here today in this Chamber, are consumers at some point in our daily lives and are therefore the whole of the Spanish population. But it is important not to see the action of consumption as a technical action, devoid of any other relevant aspect of social life. On the contrary, the act of consuming is a social relationship and, therefore, it is not a neutral act nor is it a symmetrical act, it is not done by everyone alike and it has no aseptic implications in relation, for example, to our planet. That is why this ministry, which is partly crystallizing that constitutional mandate for the first time in the history of Spanish democracy, what it establishes is the defence of the rights of consumers and, also, a series of values and principles intended to protect the most precious goods, as is, of course, our planet. Therefore, the lines that mark these budgets are trying to improve the capacity of the Government to fulfil that constitutional mandate of article 51, we insist, without budget allocation. Words are words, but in order to become facts, in practical politics, a budget, economic, is required, so that it can be deployed. It is also part of the objectives of these budgets to comply with the progressive coalition agreement; some of these elements have already been made in these months that we have been going through. And it is also an end to these budgets to meet the sustainable development goals, in particular Objective 12, which refers to the system of responsible production and consumption. And they are also - unfortunately, because of the circumstances that have affected us - objectives of this ministry to be able to manage and address the effects of the pandemic and the situations arising from them. There have been many circumstances in the area of consumption that have been affected as a result of the pandemic. I could identify, to make this exhibition easier, three broad lines that frame the vast majority of public policies that this ministry is going to carry out and that have its budgetary correlation in what we are presenting this afternoon. First of all, general consumption actions, actions in which any type of consumption, as I said, can be carried out on a daily basis. We are talking about market control and surveillance. This Ministry of Consumer Affairs is responsible for technical support for market inspection and surveillance. For example, the non-food alert network has to do with the ministry and, of course, requires budget to work well. For a very short time now, the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, through its Directorate-General, has been the liaison, the liaison office with the European Union and, therefore, we are the focal point for all national supervisory authorities. It is also important to understand that we have an analytical control over the quality and safety of non-food products. We have a laboratory to which you have been invited, whenever you want, as legitimate representatives of popular sovereignty, so that you can visit it, and which is especially important on dates like this because, for example, it is responsible for the control and safety of toys, it controls that the regulations are actually being complied with and that, for example, they are not dangerous for minors. But clothes or small appliances are also affected - I try to get down to concrete elements that on paper are a little more tedious -. And the promotion of associationism, as article 51 of the Constitution also states; therefore, the promotion of the protection of the rights of consumers: we talk about the promotion of out-of-court resolutions in relation to the conflicts arising from commercial relations established on a daily basis, we talk about improving the system of arbitration in relation to consumption and we talk about the normative development that remains ahead. It is the intention of this ministry, and of this minister in particular, that, in the coming months, an amendment to the Consumers and Users Act, which will allow for the definitive prohibition of 902 and the homogenization of the legal status of the vulnerable consumer person to facilitate its use by all public administrations, has already been announced, is already in progress and will arrive in the House, hopefully, in the coming months. Second line, healthy consumption. This is done through the Spanish Food and Nutrition Security Agency, known as Aesan, whose president is the secretary general of the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, which is responsible for promoting food security - when there is a food crisis is a fundamental institution for the control and management of this situation - and for promoting good patterns of healthy consumption or what we call nutrition; for example, the prevention of obesity. Just two months ago the Aladino 2019 report came out, which placed the data in a very clear and very worrying way: in our country, four out of ten minors between the ages of six and nine suffer some kind of overweight. And this obesity rate, worrying in itself, is especially heavy as its incidence, its prevalence is twice as high among families with fewer resources than among families with more resources, derived from patterns of daily behavior, such as physical exercise, but also and especially from patterns of consumption. These are the tasks of the agency, they are the tasks of the Ministry of Consumer Affairs. That is why we need to continue to obtain raw materials that will allow us to have a map of what is happening. In the last decade we have been fighting this situation of obesity and malnutrition as a country, two different phenomena, but in recent years there has been a significant stagnation in the indicators. Better knowledge of the situation naturally requires budget allocation for quality reports, reports that allow us to know what are the best public policies with scientific evidence, also from the point of view of food security, of course. I mentioned the food safety network earlier. The alert network operates under the Ministry of Consumer Affairs: when a product is dangerous to health, the alert network works effectively because this institution has sufficient resources and therefore we are talking about budgets. We are talking about that in the Aesan there are two additional laboratories: the National Food Centre - which I invite you to visit too - in which you can check very well the technology available to our country to deal with this problem, and the Marine Biotoxins Laboratory, which is also an important point in the European network. We have to say that they are precisely the Aesan and this space of the Ministry of Consumer Affairs a focal point within the European Food Safety Agency. Its functioning and budget allocation are therefore essential for matters which are on the day-to-day agenda of its Members: the product that is being eaten or the product that is being consumed that is not of a food nature. All this depends to a great extent on these budget headings. At the same time - we spoke before obesity - the NAOS Strategy requires resources to improve consumption habits, following scientific recommendations, for example, from the World Health Organization or the well-known European farm-to-table strategy. Having all the information and being able to carry out information, awareness, control and intervention campaigns naturally has an economic or financial need. Finally, in this area, the following regulations, which form part of the planning of the Ministry of Consumer Affairs, include frontal labelling in food. That is, when you go to the supermarket, you can see whether the product is healthy or not at a glance through a gradient of color, red to green, synthesized by an algorithm that already exists and is working in some countries, such as France - is the label called NutriScore -, which is not perfect but is an important leap forward. This will allow the user to find in a graphical way whether that product that he is going to consume is healthy or not healthy according to scientific criteria. As we always say, we don't tell people what to consume, but we do provide the scientific evidence that invites them to reflect on what patterns of consumption can have better or worse effects in the future. As we have often insisted, childhood obesity is a problem in itself, but it is also a problem because of its derivatives; for example, psychological derivatives in childhood age, but also health derivatives in adulthood. The probability of maintaining obesity in adulthood is very high when it has been lower with obesity and obesity in adulthood worsens the prognosis for very common diseases such as hypertension and also others such as, unfortunately, COVID. This frontal labelling is a contribution in addition to that comprehensive plan which we have already outlined in the Committee on Health and Consumer Affairs and in other forums too - I will therefore not be abounding in this - and which involves the management and prohibition of child advertising of certain products which are scientifically proven to worsen the quality of life of the person concerned - in this case minors - and which have very severe consequences in the medium and long term. Also, as these budgets have been relevant under other headings, there is fiscal management in line with a system of incentives and disincentives whereby healthy consumption is encouraged by lower taxes than average and unhealthy consumption is disincentived by additional items in this area. Finally, in a third line of public policies that we have to deploy we are talking about safe gambling. In the competences of this ministry comes the management of gambling and gambling. We have already completed a part of the coalition agreement by banning in a rather broad manner and with strict regulation the advertising of gambling and gambling. It was a matter that was expected in the coalition agreement after eight years without any regulation. Once we have fulfilled this step, we lack to be able to understand that there is still a functioning market, people who are participating and who can develop certain pathologies that we want to mitigate through safe playing behavior patterns, reminding all people of the reality behind, the mathematical reality, the risks to which one is exposed when participating in this type of activity, while promoting information, awareness and other campaigns to inform in a clear way what are the risks of this type of activity. As you know, in recent years there has been a great social alarm because the proliferation of gambling halls and betting houses has been widespread, especially in disadvantaged neighbourhoods, with very negative consequences for certain social groups, mainly the most vulnerable. These groups are the ones with the least resources and are those who can fall on more occasions in the temptation to believe that their situation of economic hardship could possibly be solved with a couple of clicks and with a great ability to correct or predict certain results of sporting events or any other manifestation and, consequently, that they could solve the moments of difficulty that are going through in their life. This situation is happening in our neighbourhoods and neighbourhoods and requires a public policy response which, honestly, I believe, from the Ministry of Consumer Affairs we are right to give after a long time without any regulation and which is accompanied in these budgets by a redirection - to put it mildly - or a reorientation of the rate of play so that 25% of the revenue can be used for these purposes which have to do with information, awareness, intervention, control and also reparation. I would like to point out, although it is clear, that the Directorate-General for Game Planning, which belongs to the Ministry of Consumer Affairs and is responsible for these tasks, needs a sufficient budget to maintain the basic services in place to monitor and control the activities of the game operators and to combat the fraud and damage of sports parties and events in collaboration with the National Police and the Civil Guard. Again, I invite all of you to visit the facilities of the Ministry of Consumer Affairs whenever you wish to know their high technological level and the technical and work training of our professionals and officials who are dedicated precisely to combating this type of fraud and at the same time ensure that this activity, that of gambling, which is legal in our country, can be carried out without having negative consequences for the whole population, especially for the working class, which, according to all the indicators, is very vulnerable to this type of process. In short, they are good budgets for the country and good for all those who are in this ministry. I believe that we are transforming elements of great social impact, and I say this with humility but also with clarity, and helping to eradicate some of the most structurally integrated hardships in our society. Thank you so much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u200	Thank you. We move on to the discussion of sections 32, of the Ministry of Inclusion, Social Security and Migration, and 60, Social Security. For the presentation of the sections, the Minister for Inclusion, Social Security and Migration has the floor on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u201	President, ladies and gentlemen, this is undoubtedly a budget marked by an exceptional context which has determined our performance this year and which will inevitably continue to do so as we return to normality. My first words I want to remind the victims, among whom we have ministerial workers and their families, to whom I want to send all my support from here. In the Ministry of Inclusion, Social Security and Migration we have deployed a wide range of policies aimed at alleviating the health, labour and economic consequences of this serious crisis since the first day. This budget will allow us to continue to do so next year by driving the return to normalcy of our society and economy, even though we are aware that the pandemic will also set our priorities next year. Our budget has the necessary financial tools to achieve three major objectives that we have set at the Ministry of Inclusion, Social Security and Migration since the beginning of the legislature. Firstly, the great task of social security next year will undoubtedly be to implement the recommendations of the Toledo Pact that you have just agreed in this House. Secondly, through the General Secretariat for Objectives and Policies for Social Inclusion and Social Security, we will work on the full deployment of minimum living income. Thirdly, the Secretariat of State for Migration is already moving towards a new model of migration management in line with the reality of our country. Finally, and across these objectives, the budget for 2021 includes a set of interventions aimed at improving and modernising the management of our ministry, with a strong drive for efficiency, data analysis, innovation and digitisation. A more agile administration that uses data and new technologies more efficiently is an administration with a greater capacity to respond to problems and better service to citizens. I will structure my intervention in relation to these three areas of action and start with Social Security. The State General Budgets include 172,429 million euros for Social Security, an amount that will serve to pay almost 10 million pensions and benefits as relevant as temporary incapacity, child birth allowance or minimum living income, allowing us to care for millions of citizens in their daily lives. The 2021 accounts not only finance these benefits, but also incorporate - and I would like to emphasize it very much - the pension agreement under the Toledo Pact, which was achieved here a few weeks ago with only two votes against. Four lines of action in the budgets derive from the recommendations of the compact. Firstly, by 2021, pensions will rise in line with expected inflation by 0.9 % and non-contributory pensions by 1.8 %, in line with the second recommendation of the Toledo Pact. This increase provides certainty to pensioners and reinforces the commitment of society as a whole to them and is in line with the practices of the countries in our environment. Secondly, the budgets contain a transfer of EUR 14 billion to cover the non-contributory costs of the system, those which the Toledo Pact considers should not be financed from contributions. This will allow us to visualize the real situation of social security that will be in balance at the end of the parliamentary term, as requested by the Toledo Pact. Thirdly, these budgets include a key measure to increase co-responsibility and thereby reduce the gender gap. From 1 January 2021 maternity and paternity leave shall be equal to sixteen weeks for each parent. Data show that much of the wage gap arises after the birth of the first child, and this difference is maintained for the rest of the career until pension. Finally, we have committed ourselves to strengthening our supplementary social welfare system. Current tax incentives, individual private plans, are not conducive to saving and are highly regressive. When we compare Spain with countries in our environment with similar pension systems, the underdevelopment of employment plans, collective plans, draws attention. We believe that there is room to boost savings through this route, which will benefit middle- and low-income groups as well as young people. To this end, we have included two measures in the State General Budgets: firstly, the shift from favourable taxation of individual plans to employment plans resulting from collective bargaining and, secondly, the creation of a public promotion pension fund with very small commissions, which extends to all employees and self-employed persons who wish to participate in it and, by default, by means of collective bargaining. The ultimate objective with these measures is to increase the savings of Spanish families in a simple, safe and cost-effective way. We are aware that we must strengthen the management capacity of Social Security to ensure that all these interventions are successful. We will do all this by betting on human capital and digitisation. Let me give you a series of data that I consider very relevant in this context. Over the past ten years, social security staff have fallen by 20%. 60% of our workers are over 60 years old; only 5%, less than 40 years old. And the work - as you know perfectly well - has not diminished; on the contrary, the pandemic has shown us all how important it is to have an agile public administration and how costly it is for society to decapitalize it progressively. We must certainly increase and rejuvenate our templates. To this end, we have budgeted an increase in staff expenditure of 8.5%, more than EUR 200 million, creating around 1200 new jobs in social security, which is in addition to the 270 officials we have already created new ones this year and the 1000 jobs that have also been incorporated in 2020. But, in our view, this must go hand in hand with a profound change in their structures, adapting them to a very different society than the one that Social Security had to attend to when it was designed. The impulse of digitization is unquestionable. We have already taken some steps in this direction and the pandemic crisis has, logically, forced us to do so. We are convinced that this modernization is especially valuable in order to take advantage of the enormous capacity of the administration of social security in the territory, making it a fundamental tool in the fight against depopulation and the defence of territorial cohesion. Let me now turn to the second area of action of the ministry, that of inclusion. The General Secretariat of Objectives and Policies for Inclusion and Social Security is a new body that is born with an innovative vocation: to develop the new policy of inclusion in our country. It is not only a demand from society and national experts, but also a demand from European and international institutions that for too many years have drawn our attention to the need to articulate a country policy, to protect incomes and to combat exclusion. This new policy must be pursued in the use and intensive exploitation of data through advanced processing and analysis techniques, and must also be articulated through a multi-level governance model that enables the necessary collaboration and coordination with autonomous communities, local authorities and the third sector. Our budget for 2021 will enable us to advance the work of the General Secretariat on three axes. Firstly, in the development of inclusion routes, a central element in the design of minimum living income, which aims at the transition of vulnerable groups in a situation of exclusion to full participation in social and economic life. Secondly, the success of these itineraries rests on the fact that the design contemplates the characteristics of the recipients, for which it is necessary to have very good information about the data and profiles of these people, and to evaluate their success it is necessary to implement well-designed programmes from a methodological point of view. For this reason, much of the budget allocation of this General Secretariat is geared towards the construction of metrics and the design of pilot intervention programmes in the areas of inclusion. The second priority will be the development of an inclusion map for our country. Current inclusion policies are overly atomized and we need to know better what really exists, best practices and coordinate the actions of different administrations. Finally, our third axis of action lies at the origin of the General Secretariat: the continuous evaluation and redesign of minimum living income. Minimum vital income is already a reality. We have received more than one million applications, of which almost two thirds have already processed them or interacted with their petitioners, and we have approved almost 160 000 minimum vital income. This benefit already reaches almost half a million people, reflecting unprecedented deployment compared to similar programmes in the past. In addition to continuing the effective deployment of this new benefit, it is time to raise a number of questions. Is it reaching out to all the people who need it? Is that the right amount? How are we going to encourage access to employment for the beneficiaries? The continuous assessment of minimum living income has been present from the beginning and we will adjust its design to ensure that it fulfils its task: the eradication of extreme poverty. To carry out its plan, the General Secretariat for Active Objectives and Policies for Social Inclusion and Social Security has a budget of about 120 million euros. These items will be reinforced with European funds, international programmes focusing on technical assistance and Structural Reform Support Programme projects. Let me finally address the third area of action of the ministry, that of migration. By 2021, we have set two cross-cutting objectives from the Secretariat of State for Migration: to be more effective, i.e. to have an adequate response capacity, and to be more efficient, improving management models and public resources. We have identified daily limitations on improving the current management model. Our asylum-seekers' reception system is ineffective and under-funded. Our ability to respond to changes in coastal arrival flows is clearly deficient. We have seen it in the Canary Islands, where, after years of dismantling reception capacities, we are working counterclockwise to adapt to the new needs and emergencies. The management and financing tools of the system are very unwieldy. We start from a model based on grants that outweigh the status quo and very long resolution deadlines. In addition, information and evaluation tools and administrative procedures are, of course, obsolete. The change in migration management model that we intend to implement is articulated on three main axes. The first axis is the strengthening of autonomy and capacity in the care system through a network of own centres, with special focus on the Canary Islands, where we are currently deploying around 7000 own reception places that should be available in this month of December. Secondly, we want to change our management model. The European Asylum Support Office, EASO, has recently been in Madrid making a diagnosis of the current system and suggesting proposals for improvement. In order to fulfil its recommendations, the 2021 budgets represent a transition scenario, while an EASO technical team will help us in the coming months to implement the necessary measures to achieve this transition. Finally, we will launch a digitisation plan in line with the needs of migrants in Spain and Spanish citizens abroad, which will ensure, on the one hand, an improvement in the management of the system by reducing waiting times and, on the other, greater rigour and agility in the information and processing of data. The total budget for carrying out these tasks amounts to EUR 488 million. It represents a one-third increase in actions in favour of emigrants and will be complemented by more than 40 million from the Recovery and Resilience Fund. Allow me to conclude. The year 2020 has been an exceptional year for all and our ministry has been no exception. Not only have we responded comprehensively to the harsh health, social and economic reality imposed on us by the pandemic, we have been undertaking structural reforms to strengthen our social protection system by making it more inclusive and sustainable, and always placing the focus on the most vulnerable groups. The year 2021 will be a central year in the consolidation of the transformative project of this ministry, which will have 192 million euros from the Fund for Recovery and Resilience. Our budget plan supports a triple agenda. In the area of social security, we want to assure pensioners and future pensioners of the implementation of the recommendations of the Toledo Pact. In the area of inclusion, we will launch an action plan for inclusion based on innovation in the evaluation of public policies and will fully deploy minimum vital income as a central tool. With regard to migration, we are strengthening reception, international protection and humanitarian care systems as we move towards a more flexible and responsive management model. And, of course, we will continue our policies to respond to COVID, with the aim of ensuring a rapid, vigorous and cohesive exit from the crisis over the next year. Thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen. [[Applauds]]

That of 4 December 1977 was a demonstration against memory and for sovereignty. Andalusia was well advanced in its autonomy status during the Second Republic, but was unable to complete its drafting, as Catalonia, the Basque Country and Galicia did, by the Franco coup d'état of July 1936. This mass demonstration of 1977 enabled the drafting of article 151 of the Constitution enabling the procedure - albeit infamous - by which Andalusia, after passing unjustly through the twelve tests of Asterix, obtained its status of autonomy. ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u219	Ladies and gentlemen, good afternoon. I compare with you to present the budget of the Ministry of Universities for 2021. This budget is within the framework of the General Budgets of the State which our Government presents to you. These are the most expansive budgets of our recent history that, if approved, will allow to attend to the health emergency..., [[Rumors]]
ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u220	Ladies and gentlemen, quiet, please.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-12-02-CD201202.u221	... reactivate the economy in the short term and remedy the social emergencies resulting from the impact of the pandemic. The General State Budgets proposed by the Government include a social expenditure of almost 240 billion euros, the largest in history, and consolidate the protection of the welfare state and in particular of the education system at all its academic levels. In particular, expenditure on education at all levels of 4893 million and expenditure on research, development, innovation and digitisation of 11 483 million are proposed, an increase of 70% and 80% respectively. Although this expenditure is distributed in various ministries - in particular, education, science, economic affairs - the university system, for which this ministry is responsible, benefits from all these budget lines coordinated by the Government. Thus, the scholarship programme, jointly managed by the Ministry of Education and Vocational Training and the Ministry of Universities, increases by 35% over the 22% increase already in 2020, reaching 2090 million. Approximately 40% of scholarships and 60% of scholarship expenditure correspond to higher education. Similarly, from investments in scientific and technical equipment, universities benefit from 73 % of the funds. With regard specifically to budget section 33 on the Ministry of Universities, the consolidated budget for 2021 is 494.34 million, an increase of 94.3 per cent by 2021. I would like to stress that the staff costs of this budget and of the ministry represent only 2% of the total and the current expenditure on goods and services 1%, which means that 97% of our budget is spent on expenses that directly benefit the university community as a whole, obviously not counting the funding of public universities, which is the competence of the autonomous communities and for what they have the State transfers for their total expenditure. The State General Budgets foresees an increase in transfers to the autonomous communities by 9%, which is more than 146 billion, i.e. 12,135 million more than in 2020. We assume and hope that, with these transfers, the autonomous communities will increase the funding of universities according to their criteria and within their competences, but bearing in mind that the resources of all public universities have been reduced by 21% in the last decade thanks to the policy of conservative governments. In terms of expenditure on education policies, programme 322.C, for university education, has an amount of EUR 136.13 million, i.e. a 4% increase. This includes, in particular, the new European University Programme co-financed with the European Commission and the funding of the National University of Distance Education. Our ministry has made the strategic decision to strengthen and digitize UNED, a large state university, the only one that, together with the Menéndez Pelayo International University, and while maintaining its full autonomy, has a direct link with the ministry. While UNED has always been an institution of excellence in our country, its role is now more decisive than ever because of the contribution of its semi-presential teaching methodology to the distance education imposed by the pandemic, and it also opens up a way forward for the diversification of quality-guaranteeed teaching modalities. Consequently, to the regular transfer of the 2020 budget, which was almost EUR 74 million, EUR 17 680 000 is added up to EUR 91 647 832, i.e. a 24% increase for the UNED budget. In addition, this draft State General Budgets authorizes UNED to resort to borrowing for an amount of EUR 10 million to complete the construction of the new building of the Faculty of Sciences. As regards the 323.M programme of scholarships and grants to students with a total of 39.3 million, this programme is not included in the general programme of scholarships already exposed. This includes scholarships from Menéndez Pelayo International University, national end-of-care awards dedicated to the excellence and co-financing of the Erasmus Programme which manages Sepie, under the Ministry, while maintaining the national co-financing of the Erasmus+ Programme with 30 million euros. As regards expenditure on research, development and innovation policies, as well as digitisation, universities are the main beneficiaries of the programmes convened by the State Research Agency, whose budget is, as you know, in the section on the Ministry of Science and Innovation. In addition, with regard to the section of our ministry, section 33, the following programmes should be highlighted. The 463.A programme for scientific research, with a 10% increase - i.e. 104.43 million - which includes training aids for university teachers. In 2021, according to this budget, we will increase the programme by EUR 10 million, which will allow the call for 1050 aid, i.e. 200 more than in 2020. Also, we keep the 100 assists Beatriz Galindo for the attraction of talent. The new programme 460.A, entitled research, development and innovation, within the European Funded Mechanism for Recovery and Resilience, budgeted at 203.9 million, of which we highlight the redevelopment and mobility of teaching and research staff with 129.05 million and the modernization and digitalization of the university system with 74.85 million. At the same time, we have modified in these General State Budgets the replacement rate for public universities as a result of including universities as a priority sector at the request of the Ministry. Therefore, the replacement rate for teachers and researchers is increased by 110 per cent. In addition, public universities will be able to transfer the surplus of their replacement rate to another university within the same autonomous community and the autonomous communities will be able to transfer the replacement rates that they will not use themselves to their public universities within the same community. These are essential elements to promote the necessary rejuvenation of staff. As far as external expenditure is concerned, international cooperation, promotion and dissemination of education abroad are estimated in our budget at almost EUR 2 million. With regard to the autonomous bodies, the Menéndez Pelayo International University, which plays a fundamental role in the intellectual dynamism of our society, receives an increase of 16.8% for operating expenses, up to a total budget of 24,882,000 euros. Sepie, the international branch of the ministry, increases the budget by 14%, reaching 42,640,000 euros. In this context, we maintain national co-financing with the European Commission of the Erasmus+ programme. And Aneca, the autonomous agency for the evaluation and accreditation of teachers, programmes and titles, receives an increase of 2.69%, until reaching a budget of 12,578,750 euros. Ladies and gentlemen, thank you for your attention. [[Applauds]]

This new programme of research, development and innovation of the Recovery Mechanism also lays the foundations for modernization and digitisation as axes of the Spanish university system. There are nearly €70 million in aid in this area, also in the boost to the international retraining and mobility of teaching staff and university researchers. Of these two games, 10 million will also be for UNED, because UNED is also at the centre and heart of the ministry's policies. Universities have a lot to contribute to this country. These ministries provide the basis for these dynamic, active and committed universities to contribute everything they have to bring to the change of our productive system. Gentlemen of the PP, was there really no argument to justify at least thinking about your vote in favour of these budgets? These budgets double the number of universities; they will go ahead despite you, and with them we will continue to strengthen the equal opportunities that makes the Spanish university system one of the most equitable in the world. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]
